What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is I don’t feel like myself anymore—I barely talk to people, I just go flat and quiet, and even getting started on the cleaning route feels like pushing a loaded cart uphill.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what brought me in is… I don’t feel like myself anymore. It’s hard to explain without making it sound like I’m just tired or having a bad week, but it’s more than that. I barely talk to people now, even when I’m around them. I used to at least say a few things, joke a little maybe, but lately I just go kind of flat and quiet, like there’s nothing in me to push the words out.

And at work, doing the cleaning route, even getting started feels like pushing a loaded cart uphill. I’ll know what needs doing, I’ll be standing there with the supplies, but it’s like my body and my mind won’t get moving the way they should. I just feel slowed down and shut off, and it worries me because that’s not how I used to be.

It’s been… a while. Not just a few weeks or something. I’d say it’s been building up for about a year and five months now, give or take. It kind of started after I had to leave my old apartment and the neighborhood I was used to.

Not really, no. That’s one of the things that bothers me, actually, because I can remember what it used to feel like to want to do things.

Like, Saturdays I used to look forward to going fishing. Even if I didn’t catch much, I liked getting my gear together, being by the water, having that quiet. Or in the morning I’d sit with coffee and do crosswords, and that used to feel kind of satisfying, like getting my brain warmed up. Even at work, I used to like showing the younger guys little tricks, you know, how to strip a floor the right way without making a mess of it, how to save time if you know what you’re doing.

Now I mostly just let all that sit. The fishing stuff is there, the crossword book is there, the guys at work ask things sometimes, but it doesn’t give me much back. It’s like I remember that I used to enjoy it, but I don’t feel the enjoyment now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.

I keep going back to losing my old place, like that was the point where I proved I couldn’t keep my life together. I know people can tell me circumstances happen, rent changes, things pile up, whatever, but in my head it still comes back to, “You failed. You should’ve handled it better.” And it’s not just about the apartment itself — it feels like I failed everybody somehow, like I let my whole life slip out of my hands.

And then it spreads to things that maybe don’t even make sense. Like if the church pantry is short on supplies, I’ll catch myself thinking I should’ve done something, like I should’ve noticed or helped more, even if it wasn’t really my responsibility. Or at work, if a hallway gets missed or something isn’t cleaned right, I take it personal, like that’s on me, like I’m the reason things fall apart.

It’s tiring, because even when nobody’s blaming me out loud, I’m already doing it in my own head.

that should normally be present are diminished or lost.\n  manifestation: What brought me in is I don’t feel like myself anymore—I barely talk to people, I just go flat and quiet, and even getting those things sit because they don’t give me anything back.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: Most days food just tastes like cardboard, so I skip meals or eat a few crackers after my shift, and my work pants have been hanging looser than they used to.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: Even if I sleep through the night, my arms and legs feel heavy by the time I get to the building, and small jobs like hauling trash bags wear me out more than they should.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I failed everybody by losing my old place and not keeping my life together, like if the church pantry is short on supplies or a hallway gets missed, somehow that’s on me too.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have some big plan, but a lot of mornings I wake up thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all, and that scares me enough to say it out loud.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I was at my lowest—barely eating, feeling guilty, and thinking I might not want to wake up—I was also hearing that man’s voice at work and believing people in the building were watching me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: mind.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had lifted some and I was getting through meals and work a little better, but I still heard my name in the stairwell and stayed convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My supervisor started writing up missed routes and repeated cleaning mistakes, and I stopped keeping up with church, fishing, and the people I used to check in on. I finally went to the employee assistance program because I could not keep my work routine straight anymore.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been building for about 1 year and 5 months, ever since I had to leave my old apartment and neighborhood.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The voices and worries about being watched have been going on for about 11 months now.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The depression part has been with me for about 1 year and 2 months, starting not long after the move.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. Not just a few weeks or something. I’d say it’s been building up for about a year and five months now, give or take. Or in the morning I’d sit with coffee and do crosswords, and that used to feel kind of satisfying, like getting my brain warmed up. Even at work, I used to like showing the younger guys little tricks, you know, how to strip a floor the right way without making a mess of it, how to save time if you know what you’re doing.\n\nNow I mostly just let all that sit. The fishing stuff is there, the crossword book is there, the guys at work ask things sometimes, but it doesn’t give me much back. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nI keep going back to losing my old place, like that was the point where I proved I couldn’t keep my life together. I know people can tell me circumstances happen, rent changes, things pile up, whatever, but in my head it still comes back to, “You failed. You should’ve handled it better.” And it’s not just about the apartment itself — it feels like I failed everybody somehow, like I let my whole life slip out of my hands.\n\nAnd then it spreads to things that maybe don’t even make sense. Like if the church pantry is short on supplies, I’ll catch myself thinking I should’ve done something, like I should’ve noticed or helped more, even if it wasn’t really my responsibility. Or at work, if a hallway gets missed or something isn’t cleaned right, I take it personal, like that’s on me, like I’m the reason things fall apart.\n\nIt’s tiring, because even when nobody’s blaming me out loud, I’m already doing it in my own head.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that, but yes.

It’s not like I’ve got some big plan written out or anything like that. pantry is short on supplies or a hallway gets missed, somehow that’s on me too.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have some big plan, but a lot of mornings I wake up thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all, and that scares me enough to say it out loud.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I was at my lowest—barely eating, feeling guilty, and thinking I might not want to wake up—I was also hearing that man’s voice at work and believing people in the building were watching me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: For most of the time since the move, the sadness, low energy, guilt, and loss of interest have been there in the background, even on days when the voices or camera worries were the main thing on my manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had lifted some and I was getting through meals and work a little better, but I still heard my name in the stairwell and stayed convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My supervisor started writing up missed routes and repeated cleaning mistakes, and I stopped keeping up with church, fishing, and the people I used to check in on. That’s one of the things that bothers me, actually, because I can remember what it used to feel like to want to do things.\n\nLike, Saturdays I used to look forward to going fishing. Even if I didn’t catch much, I liked getting my gear together, being by the water, having that quiet. Or in the morning I’d sit with coffee and do crosswords, and that used to feel kind of satisfying, like getting my brain warmed up. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. Mostly hearing, not so much seeing things.

It happens at work, usually when I’m mopping after hours and the building’s mostly empty. I’ll be going down the hallway or near the stairs, and I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name. Clear. Not like a thought in my head exactly — more like somebody standing over by the stairwell said it.

Sometimes it’ll be something like, “You missed a spot,” and I’ll turn around thinking maybe one of the guys stayed late, or a supervisor came back in, but there’s nobody there. I’ll check around the corner, look down the stairs, and it’s empty. And it’s not just the building noises, at least it doesn’t feel like that when it happens. It sounds like a person.

I don’t really like talking about it because it makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. But yes, I’ve heard things other people don’t seem to hear.

days food just tastes like cardboard, so I skip meals or eat a few crackers after my shift, and my work pants have been hanging looser than they used to.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: Even if I sleep through the night, my arms and legs feel heavy by the time I get to the building, and small jobs like hauling trash bags wear me out more than they should.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I failed everybody by losing my old place and not keeping my life together, like if the church pantry is short on supplies or a hallway gets missed, somehow that’s on me too.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have some big plan, but a lot of mornings I wake up thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all, and that scares me enough to say it out loud.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I was at my lowest—barely eating, feeling guilty, and thinking I might not want to wake up—I was also hearing that man’s voice at work and believing people in the building were watching me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: For most of the time since the move, the sadness, low energy, guilt, and loss of interest have been there in the background, even on days when the voices or camera worries were the main thing on my mind.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had lifted some and I was getting through meals and work a little better, but I still heard my name in the stairwell and stayed convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My supervisor started writing up missed routes and repeated cleaning mistakes, and I stopped keeping up with church, fishing, and the people I used to check in on. I finally went to the employee assistance program because I could not keep my work routine straight anymore.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been building for about 1 year and 5 months, ever since I had to leave my old apartment and neighborhood.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The voices and worries about being watched have been going on for about 11 months now.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The depression part has been with me for about 1 year and 2 months, starting not long after the move.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nI keep going back to losing my old place, like that was the point where I proved I couldn’t keep my life together. I know people can tell me circumstances happen, rent changes, things pile up, whatever, but in my head it still comes back to, “You failed. You should’ve handled it better.” And it’s not just about the apartment itself — it feels like I failed everybody somehow, like I let my whole life slip out of my hands.\n\nAnd then it spreads to things that maybe don’t even make sense. Like if the church pantry is short on supplies, I’ll catch myself thinking I should’ve done something, like I should’ve noticed or helped more, even if it wasn’t really my responsibility. Or at work, if a hallway gets missed or something isn’t cleaned right, I take it personal, like that’s on me, like I’m the reason things fall apart.\n\nIt’s tiring, because even when nobody’s blaming me out loud, I’m already doing it in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve got some big plan written out or anything like that. It’s more… a lot of mornings, when I wake up, before I even really get moving, there’s this thought that it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Like if I could’ve just stayed asleep and not had to face another day.\n\nAnd then I get scared because I hear myself thinking that, and part of me knows that’s not a normal thing to be carrying around. I haven’t really wanted to say it out loud, because once you say it, it feels serious, you know? But it’s been there enough that… yeah, I think I should tell you.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly hearing, not so much seeing things.\n\nIt happens at work, usually when I’m mopping after hours and the building’s mostly empty. I’ll be going down the hallway or near the stairs, and I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name. Clear. Not like a thought in my head exactly — more like somebody standing over by the stairwell said it.\n\nSometimes it’ll be something like, “You missed a spot,” and I’ll turn around thinking maybe one of the guys stayed late, or a supervisor came back in, but there’s nobody there. I’ll check around the corner, look down the stairs, and it’s empty. And it’s not just the building noises, at least it doesn’t feel like that when it happens. It sounds like a person.\n\nI don’t really like talking about it because it makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. But yes, I’ve heard things other people don’t seem to hear.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever seen things or had visions that other people couldn't see?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really visions, no… not like seeing a person standing there or seeing shapes or anything that I can point to with my eyes.

What happens to me is more with hearing. Like I was saying, when I’m mopping after hours and the place is mostly empty, I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name, or say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it’s clear enough that I stop what I’m doing and look over toward the stairwell or down the hallway, because it feels like somebody is actually there. But then I check and there’s nobody.

So I wouldn’t say I’m having visions, exactly. It’s more that I hear things that seem real in the moment, and then when I look, there’s no one there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… there was a time like that. Not perfect, not like I was suddenly myself again, but better than the worst of it.

It was maybe several weeks where the heaviness lifted some. I was eating a little more normally, getting through meals without it feeling like a chore every single time, and work didn’t feel quite as impossible. I could show up, do the floors, get through the trash, mop the hallways, and not feel like I was dragging a weight behind me every minute.

But the strange part is, even during that stretch, the other stuff didn’t really go away. I’d still hear my name by the stairwell sometimes, clear enough that I’d stop and look. And I was still pretty convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track where I was going, like somebody was watching me through them more than they should’ve been.

So, yes, I had a period where my mood was better and I was functioning a little better, but I don’t think everything went back to normal. Some things stayed there even when I wasn’t as down.

I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve got some big plan written out or anything like that. It’s more… a lot of mornings, when I wake up, before I even really get moving, there’s this thought that it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Like if I could’ve just stayed asleep and not had to face another day.\n\nAnd then I get scared because I hear myself thinking that, and part of me knows that’s not a normal thing to be carrying around. I haven’t really wanted to say it out loud, because once you say it, it feels serious, you know? But it’s been there enough that… yeah, I think I should tell you.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly hearing, not so much seeing things.\n\nIt happens at work, usually when I’m mopping after hours and the building’s mostly empty. I’ll be going down the hallway or near the stairs, and I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name. Clear. Not like a thought in my head exactly — more like somebody standing over by the stairwell said it.\n\nSometimes it’ll be something like, “You missed a spot,” and I’ll turn around thinking maybe one of the guys stayed late, or a supervisor came back in, but there’s nobody there. I’ll check around the corner, look down the stairs, and it’s empty. And it’s not just the building noises, at least it doesn’t feel like that when it happens. It sounds like a person.\n\nI don’t really like talking about it because it makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. But yes, I’ve heard things other people don’t seem to hear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things or had visions that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Not really visions, no… not like seeing a person standing there or seeing shapes or anything that I can point to with my eyes.\n\nWhat happens to me is more with hearing. Like I was saying, when I’m mopping after hours and the place is mostly empty, I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name, or say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it’s clear enough that I stop what I’m doing and look over toward the stairwell or down the hallway, because it feels like somebody is actually there. But then I check and there’s nobody.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m having visions, exactly. It’s more that I hear things that seem real in the moment, and then when I look, there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt better, almost back to normal, between these episodes of feeling down and slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a time like that. Not perfect, not like I was suddenly myself again, but better than the worst of it.\n\nIt was maybe several weeks where the heaviness lifted some. I was eating a little more normally, getting through meals without it feeling like a chore every single time, and work didn’t feel quite as impossible. I could show up, do the floors, get through the trash, mop the hallways, and not feel like I was dragging a weight behind me every minute.\n\nBut the strange part is, even during that stretch, the other stuff didn’t really go away. I’d still hear my name by the stairwell sometimes, clear enough that I’d stop and look. And I was still pretty convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track where I was going, like somebody was watching me through them more than they should’ve been.\n\nSo, yes, I had a period where my mood was better and I was functioning a little better, but I don’t think everything went back to normal. Some things stayed there even when I wasn’t as down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’d say it was several weeks… I’m not great with exact dates, but it wasn’t just a day or two. It was long enough that I noticed I wasn’t quite as buried under it.

No… no, they didn’t really stop. That’s the part that bothered me, actually, because even when I was feeling a little better mood-wise, I’d still have those moments.

Like if I was mopping after hours and it was quiet, I’d hear a man’s voice say my name, or sometimes say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it wasn’t like a thought in my head. It sounded clear, like somebody was standing over by the stairwell or just around the corner. I’d stop what I was doing, look up, sometimes even walk over a bit to check, and there wouldn’t be anyone there.

So during those several weeks, it might’ve felt a little easier to get through the day, but the voices were still happening. They didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as down.

Or in the morning I’d sit with coffee and do crosswords, and that used to feel kind of satisfying, like getting my brain warmed up. Even at work, I used to like showing the younger guys little tricks, you know, how to strip a floor the right way without making a mess of it, how to save time if you know what you’re doing.\n\nNow I mostly just let all that sit. The fishing stuff is there, the crossword book is there, the guys at work ask things sometimes, but it doesn’t give me much back. It’s like I remember that I used to enjoy it, but I don’t feel the enjoyment now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nI keep going back to losing my old place, like that was the point where I proved I couldn’t keep my life together. I know people can tell me circumstances happen, rent changes, things pile up, whatever, but in my head it still comes back to, “You failed. You should’ve handled it better.” And it’s not just about the apartment itself — it feels like I failed everybody somehow, like I let my whole life slip out of my hands.\n\nAnd then it spreads to things that maybe don’t even make sense. Like if the church pantry is short on supplies, I’ll catch myself thinking I should’ve done something, like I should’ve noticed or helped more, even if it wasn’t really my responsibility. Or at work, if a hallway gets missed or something isn’t cleaned right, I take it personal, like that’s on me, like I’m the reason things fall apart.\n\nIt’s tiring, because even when nobody’s blaming me out loud, I’m already doing it in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve got some big plan written out or anything like that. It’s more… a lot of mornings, when I wake up, before I even really get moving, there’s this thought that it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Like if I could’ve just stayed asleep and not had to face another day.\n\nAnd then I get scared because I hear myself thinking that, and part of me knows that’s not a normal thing to be carrying around. I haven’t really wanted to say it out loud, because once you say it, it feels serious, you know? But it’s been there enough that… yeah, I think I should tell you.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly hearing, not so much seeing things.\n\nIt happens at work, usually when I’m mopping after hours and the building’s mostly empty. I’ll be going down the hallway or near the stairs, and I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name. Clear. Not like a thought in my head exactly — more like somebody standing over by the stairwell said it.\n\nSometimes it’ll be something like, “You missed a spot,” and I’ll turn around thinking maybe one of the guys stayed late, or a supervisor came back in, but there’s nobody there. I’ll check around the corner, look down the stairs, and it’s empty. And it’s not just the building noises, at least it doesn’t feel like that when it happens. It sounds like a person.\n\nI don’t really like talking about it because it makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. But yes, I’ve heard things other people don’t seem to hear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things or had visions that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Not really visions, no… not like seeing a person standing there or seeing shapes or anything that I can point to with my eyes.\n\nWhat happens to me is more with hearing. Like I was saying, when I’m mopping after hours and the place is mostly empty, I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name, or say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it’s clear enough that I stop what I’m doing and look over toward the stairwell or down the hallway, because it feels like somebody is actually there. But then I check and there’s nobody.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m having visions, exactly. It’s more that I hear things that seem real in the moment, and then when I look, there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt better, almost back to normal, between these episodes of feeling down and slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a time like that. Not perfect, not like I was suddenly myself again, but better than the worst of it.\n\nIt was maybe several weeks where the heaviness lifted some. I was eating a little more normally, getting through meals without it feeling like a chore every single time, and work didn’t feel quite as impossible. I could show up, do the floors, get through the trash, mop the hallways, and not feel like I was dragging a weight behind me every minute.\n\nBut the strange part is, even during that stretch, the other stuff didn’t really go away. I’d still hear my name by the stairwell sometimes, clear enough that I’d stop and look. And I was still pretty convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track where I was going, like somebody was watching me through them more than they should’ve been.\n\nSo, yes, I had a period where my mood was better and I was functioning a little better, but I don’t think everything went back to normal. Some things stayed there even when I wasn’t as down.\n\nDoctor: How long did that several weeks period where you felt better last?\n\nPatient: I’d say it was several weeks… I’m not great with exact dates, but it wasn’t just a day or two. It was long enough that I noticed I wasn’t quite as buried under it.\n\nDuring that stretch my mood had lifted some — not all the way, but enough that meals were a little easier and I could get through work a bit better. Like I could do the mopping and the hallways and get through the shift without feeling completely slowed down every minute.\n\nBut even then, the other things were still there. I’d still hear my name around the stairwell sometimes, and I still felt pretty sure the hallway cameras were being used to track where I was going. So the “better” period was several weeks, but it wasn’t like everything cleared up.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices you heard stop or go away during that several weeks period when you felt better?\n\nPatient: No… no, they didn’t really stop. That’s the part that bothered me, actually, because even when I was feeling a little better mood-wise, I’d still have those moments.\n\nLike if I was mopping after hours and it was quiet, I’d hear a man’s voice say my name, or sometimes say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it wasn’t like a thought in my head. It sounded clear, like somebody was standing over by the stairwell or just around the corner. I’d stop what I was doing, look up, sometimes even walk over a bit to check, and there wouldn’t be anyone there.\n\nSo during those several weeks, it might’ve felt a little easier to get through the day, but the voices were still happening. They didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Some things stayed there even when I wasn’t as down.\n\nDoctor: How long did that several weeks period where you felt better last?\n\nPatient: I’d say it was several weeks… I’m not great with exact dates, but it wasn’t just a day or two. It was long enough that I noticed I wasn’t quite as buried under it.\n\nDuring that stretch my mood had lifted some — not all the way, but enough that meals were a little easier and I could get through work a bit better. Like I could do the mopping and the hallways and get through the shift without feeling completely slowed down every minute.\n\nBut even then, the other things were still there. I’d still hear my name around the stairwell sometimes, and I still felt pretty sure the hallway cameras were being used to track where I was going. So the “better” period was several weeks, but it wasn’t like everything cleared up.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices you heard stop or go away during that several weeks period when you felt better?\n\nPatient: No… no, they didn’t really stop. That’s the part that bothered me, actually, because even when I was feeling a little better mood-wise, I’d still have those moments.\n\nLike if I was mopping after hours and it was quiet, I’d hear a man’s voice say my name, or sometimes say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it wasn’t like a thought in my head. It sounded clear, like somebody was standing over by the stairwell or just around the corner. I’d stop what I was doing, look up, sometimes even walk over a bit to check, and there wouldn’t be anyone there.\n\nSo during those several weeks, it might’ve felt a little easier to get through the day, but the voices were still happening. They didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as down.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid the voices you heard or the feeling of being watched ever completely stop, even for a short period, when you weren't feeling down?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 3\nmanifestation: \"There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had lifted some and I was getting through meals and work a little better, but I still heard my name in the stairwell and stayed convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think it ever completely stopped. That’s what I keep coming back to, because there was that stretch — a few weeks, I guess — where I wasn’t as low as I had been. I was eating a little better, not sitting there staring at the plate like it was some impossible thing, and I could get through work more normally. I mean, I was still tired, but I could do the halls, the trash, the mopping, without feeling like every movement took everything I had.

But the voices and the watching feeling… those were still there. I’d still hear my name near the stairwell sometimes, clear enough that I’d stop and look around. And I still believed the hallway cameras were tracking me, like someone was checking where I went. So even when my mood lifted some, I can’t say those things went away, not completely.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, that happens sometimes. It’s embarrassing, because I know the answer should be simple, and I can kind of hear myself not giving the simple answer.

Like my supervisor will ask, “Which floors did you finish?” and I mean, the answer is supposed to be, “I did the second and third,” or whatever it is. But instead I’ll start explaining how I went down the old bus route first, or I’ll mention the garden plot behind where I used to live, and then somehow I’m talking about the church basement because it reminds me of the hallway smell or the floor wax, and I can tell his face is changing like he’s waiting for me to come back around.

And I do know I’m not answering right away. That’s the strange part. I can hear myself going off to the side, like I’m walking away from the question while I’m still talking. I try to pull it back, but sometimes it takes me a minute to get to, “Oh, right, I finished these floors.” So yes, sometimes it’s hard to get my thoughts out in a way that makes sense to people.

I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve got some big plan written out or anything like that. I’ll check around the corner, look down the stairs, and it’s empty. And it’s not just the building noises, at least it doesn’t feel like that when it happens. It sounds like a person.\n\nI don’t really like talking about it because it makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. But yes, I’ve heard things other people don’t seem to hear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things or had visions that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Not really visions, no… not like seeing a person standing there or seeing shapes or anything that I can point to with my eyes.\n\nWhat happens to me is more with hearing. Like I was saying, when I’m mopping after hours and the place is mostly empty, I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name, or say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it’s clear enough that I stop what I’m doing and look over toward the stairwell or down the hallway, because it feels like somebody is actually there. But then I check and there’s nobody.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m having visions, exactly. Like if the church pantry is short on supplies, I’ll catch myself thinking I should’ve done something, like I should’ve noticed or helped more, even if it wasn’t really my responsibility. Or at work, if a hallway gets missed or something isn’t cleaned right, I take it personal, like that’s on me, like I’m the reason things fall apart.\n\nIt’s tiring, because even when nobody’s blaming me out loud, I’m already doing it in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve got some big plan written out or anything like that. It’s more… a lot of mornings, when I wake up, before I even really get moving, there’s this thought that it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Like if I could’ve just stayed asleep and not had to face another day.\n\nAnd then I get scared because I hear myself thinking that, and part of me knows that’s not a normal thing to be carrying around. I haven’t really wanted to say it out loud, because once you say it, it feels serious, you know? But it’s been there enough that… yeah, I think I should tell you.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly hearing, not so much seeing things.\n\nIt happens at work, usually when I’m mopping after hours and the building’s mostly empty. I’ll be going down the hallway or near the stairs, and I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name. Clear. Not like a thought in my head exactly — more like somebody standing over by the stairwell said it.\n\nSometimes it’ll be something like, “You missed a spot,” and I’ll turn around thinking maybe one of the guys stayed late, or a supervisor came back in, but there’s nobody there. I’ll check around the corner, look down the stairs, and it’s empty. And it’s not just the building noises, at least it doesn’t feel like that when it happens. It sounds like a person.\n\nI don’t really like talking about it because it makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. But yes, I’ve heard things other people don’t seem to hear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things or had visions that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Not really visions, no… not like seeing a person standing there or seeing shapes or anything that I can point to with my eyes.\n\nWhat happens to me is more with hearing. Like I was saying, when I’m mopping after hours and the place is mostly empty, I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name, or say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it’s clear enough that I stop what I’m doing and look over toward the stairwell or down the hallway, because it feels like somebody is actually there. But then I check and there’s nobody.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m having visions, exactly. It’s more that I hear things that seem real in the moment, and then when I look, there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt better, almost back to normal, between these episodes of feeling down and slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a time like that. Not perfect, not like I was suddenly myself again, but better than the worst of it.\n\nIt was maybe several weeks where the heaviness lifted some. I was eating a little more normally, getting through meals without it feeling like a chore every single time, and work didn’t feel quite as impossible. I could show up, do the floors, get through the trash, mop the hallways, and not feel like I was dragging a weight behind me every minute.\n\nBut the strange part is, even during that stretch, the other stuff didn’t really go away. I’d still hear my name by the stairwell sometimes, clear enough that I’d stop and look. And I was still pretty convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track where I was going, like somebody was watching me through them more than they should’ve been.\n\nSo, yes, I had a period where my mood was better and I was functioning a little better, but I don’t think everything went back to normal. Some things stayed there even when I wasn’t as down.\n\nDoctor: How long did that several weeks period where you felt better last?\n\nPatient: I’d say it was several weeks… I’m not great with exact dates, but it wasn’t just a day or two. It was long enough that I noticed I wasn’t quite as buried under it.\n\nDuring that stretch my mood had lifted some — not all the way, but enough that meals were a little easier and I could get through work a bit better. Like I could do the mopping and the hallways and get through the shift without feeling completely slowed down every minute.\n\nBut even then, the other things were still there. I’d still hear my name around the stairwell sometimes, and I still felt pretty sure the hallway cameras were being used to track where I was going. So the “better” period was several weeks, but it wasn’t like everything cleared up.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices you heard stop or go away during that several weeks period when you felt better?\n\nPatient: No… no, they didn’t really stop. That’s the part that bothered me, actually, because even when I was feeling a little better mood-wise, I’d still have those moments.\n\nLike if I was mopping after hours and it was quiet, I’d hear a man’s voice say my name, or sometimes say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it wasn’t like a thought in my head. It sounded clear, like somebody was standing over by the stairwell or just around the corner. I’d stop what I was doing, look up, sometimes even walk over a bit to check, and there wouldn’t be anyone there.\n\nSo during those several weeks, it might’ve felt a little easier to get through the day, but the voices were still happening. They didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as down.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices you heard or the feeling of being watched ever completely stop, even for a short period, when you weren't feeling down?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it ever completely stopped. That’s what I keep coming back to, because there was that stretch — a few weeks, I guess — where I wasn’t as low as I had been. I was eating a little better, not sitting there staring at the plate like it was some impossible thing, and I could get through work more normally. I mean, I was still tired, but I could do the halls, the trash, the mopping, without feeling like every movement took everything I had.\n\nBut the voices and the watching feeling… those were still there. I’d still hear my name near the stairwell sometimes, clear enough that I’d stop and look around. And I still believed the hallway cameras were tracking me, like someone was checking where I went. So even when my mood lifted some, I can’t say those things went away, not completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feelings in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that happens sometimes. It’s embarrassing, because I know the answer should be simple, and I can kind of hear myself not giving the simple answer.\n\nLike my supervisor will ask, “Which floors did you finish?” and I mean, the answer is supposed to be, “I did the second and third,” or whatever it is. But instead I’ll start explaining how I went down the old bus route first, or I’ll mention the garden plot behind where I used to live, and then somehow I’m talking about the church basement because it reminds me of the hallway smell or the floor wax, and I can tell his face is changing like he’s waiting for me to come back around.\n\nAnd I do know I’m not answering right away. That’s the strange part. I can hear myself going off to the side, like I’m walking away from the question while I’m still talking. I try to pull it back, but sometimes it takes me a minute to get to, “Oh, right, I finished these floors.” So yes, sometimes it’s hard to get my thoughts out in a way that makes sense to people.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. Not every single time, but often enough that people notice, I think. It’s like I start with the question they asked me, but then my mind catches on something next to it, and I follow that instead.

Like with my supervisor, he’ll ask something simple, like which floors I finished. And I know what he needs from me — just a straight answer, second floor, third floor, whatever. But I’ll start talking about how I got there, or I’ll mention the old bus route for some reason, then the garden plot behind where I used to live, and then somehow I’m talking about the church basement because the hallway smell reminds me of it. And while I’m saying it, part of me knows, “You’re not answering him. You’re not getting back to the point.”

I can see it on his face too, like he’s waiting for the actual answer. Then I feel kind of foolish and try to pull myself back around. So yes, I do have trouble staying on topic sometimes. It’s not that I’m trying to avoid the question. It just… goes sideways before I can stop it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brought me in is… I don’t feel like myself anymore. You should’ve handled it better.” And it’s not just about the apartment itself — it feels like I failed everybody somehow, like I let my whole life slip out of my hands.\n\nAnd then it spreads to things that maybe don’t even make sense. Like if the church pantry is short on supplies, I’ll catch myself thinking I should’ve done something, like I should’ve noticed or helped more, even if it wasn’t really my responsibility. Or at work, if a hallway gets missed or something isn’t cleaned right, I take it personal, like that’s on me, like I’m the reason things fall apart.\n\nIt’s tiring, because even when nobody’s blaming me out loud, I’m already doing it in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve got some big plan written out or anything like that. It’s more… a lot of mornings, when I wake up, before I even really get moving, there’s this thought that it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Like if I could’ve just stayed asleep and not had to face another day.\n\nAnd then I get scared because I hear myself thinking that, and part of me knows that’s not a normal thing to be carrying around. I haven’t really wanted to say it out loud, because once you say it, it feels serious, you know? But it’s been there enough that… yeah, I think I should tell you.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Mostly hearing, not so much seeing things.\n\nIt happens at work, usually when I’m mopping after hours and the building’s mostly empty. I’ll be going down the hallway or near the stairs, and I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name. Clear. Not like a thought in my head exactly — more like somebody standing over by the stairwell said it.\n\nSometimes it’ll be something like, “You missed a spot,” and I’ll turn around thinking maybe one of the guys stayed late, or a supervisor came back in, but there’s nobody there. I’ll check around the corner, look down the stairs, and it’s empty. And it’s not just the building noises, at least it doesn’t feel like that when it happens. It sounds like a person.\n\nI don’t really like talking about it because it makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. But yes, I’ve heard things other people don’t seem to hear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things or had visions that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Not really visions, no… not like seeing a person standing there or seeing shapes or anything that I can point to with my eyes.\n\nWhat happens to me is more with hearing. Like I was saying, when I’m mopping after hours and the place is mostly empty, I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name, or say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it’s clear enough that I stop what I’m doing and look over toward the stairwell or down the hallway, because it feels like somebody is actually there. But then I check and there’s nobody.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m having visions, exactly. It’s more that I hear things that seem real in the moment, and then when I look, there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt better, almost back to normal, between these episodes of feeling down and slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a time like that. Not perfect, not like I was suddenly myself again, but better than the worst of it.\n\nIt was maybe several weeks where the heaviness lifted some. I was eating a little more normally, getting through meals without it feeling like a chore every single time, and work didn’t feel quite as impossible. I could show up, do the floors, get through the trash, mop the hallways, and not feel like I was dragging a weight behind me every minute.\n\nBut the strange part is, even during that stretch, the other stuff didn’t really go away. I’d still hear my name by the stairwell sometimes, clear enough that I’d stop and look. And I was still pretty convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track where I was going, like somebody was watching me through them more than they should’ve been.\n\nSo, yes, I had a period where my mood was better and I was functioning a little better, but I don’t think everything went back to normal. Some things stayed there even when I wasn’t as down.\n\nDoctor: How long did that several weeks period where you felt better last?\n\nPatient: I’d say it was several weeks… I’m not great with exact dates, but it wasn’t just a day or two. It was long enough that I noticed I wasn’t quite as buried under it.\n\nDuring that stretch my mood had lifted some — not all the way, but enough that meals were a little easier and I could get through work a bit better. Like I could do the mopping and the hallways and get through the shift without feeling completely slowed down every minute.\n\nBut even then, the other things were still there. I’d still hear my name around the stairwell sometimes, and I still felt pretty sure the hallway cameras were being used to track where I was going. So the “better” period was several weeks, but it wasn’t like everything cleared up.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices you heard stop or go away during that several weeks period when you felt better?\n\nPatient: No… no, they didn’t really stop. That’s the part that bothered me, actually, because even when I was feeling a little better mood-wise, I’d still have those moments.\n\nLike if I was mopping after hours and it was quiet, I’d hear a man’s voice say my name, or sometimes say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it wasn’t like a thought in my head. It sounded clear, like somebody was standing over by the stairwell or just around the corner. I’d stop what I was doing, look up, sometimes even walk over a bit to check, and there wouldn’t be anyone there.\n\nSo during those several weeks, it might’ve felt a little easier to get through the day, but the voices were still happening. They didn’t go away just because I wasn’t as down.\n\nDoctor: Did the voices you heard or the feeling of being watched ever completely stop, even for a short period, when you weren't feeling down?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it ever completely stopped. That’s what I keep coming back to, because there was that stretch — a few weeks, I guess — where I wasn’t as low as I had been. I was eating a little better, not sitting there staring at the plate like it was some impossible thing, and I could get through work more normally. I mean, I was still tired, but I could do the halls, the trash, the mopping, without feeling like every movement took everything I had.\n\nBut the voices and the watching feeling… those were still there. I’d still hear my name near the stairwell sometimes, clear enough that I’d stop and look around. And I still believed the hallway cameras were tracking me, like someone was checking where I went. So even when my mood lifted some, I can’t say those things went away, not completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feelings in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that happens sometimes. It’s embarrassing, because I know the answer should be simple, and I can kind of hear myself not giving the simple answer.\n\nLike my supervisor will ask, “Which floors did you finish?” and I mean, the answer is supposed to be, “I did the second and third,” or whatever it is. But instead I’ll start explaining how I went down the old bus route first, or I’ll mention the garden plot behind where I used to live, and then somehow I’m talking about the church basement because it reminds me of the hallway smell or the floor wax, and I can tell his face is changing like he’s waiting for me to come back around.\n\nAnd I do know I’m not answering right away. That’s the strange part. I can hear myself going off to the side, like I’m walking away from the question while I’m still talking. I try to pull it back, but sometimes it takes me a minute to get to, “Oh, right, I finished these floors.” So yes, sometimes it’s hard to get my thoughts out in a way that makes sense to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble staying on topic when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Not every single time, but often enough that people notice, I think. It’s like I start with the question they asked me, but then my mind catches on something next to it, and I follow that instead.\n\nLike with my supervisor, he’ll ask something simple, like which floors I finished. And I know what he needs from me — just a straight answer, second floor, third floor, whatever. But I’ll start talking about how I got there, or I’ll mention the old bus route for some reason, then the garden plot behind where I used to live, and then somehow I’m talking about the church basement because the hallway smell reminds me of it. And while I’m saying it, part of me knows, “You’re not answering him. You’re not getting back to the point.”\n\nI can see it on his face too, like he’s waiting for the actual answer. Then I feel kind of foolish and try to pull myself back around. So yes, I do have trouble staying on topic sometimes. It’s not that I’m trying to avoid the question. It just… goes sideways before I can stop it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s embarrassing, because I know the answer should be simple, and I can kind of hear myself not giving the simple answer.\n\nLike my supervisor will ask, “Which floors did you finish?” and I mean, the answer is supposed to be, “I did the second and third,” or whatever it is. But instead I’ll start explaining how I went down the old bus route first, or I’ll mention the garden plot behind where I used to live, and then somehow I’m talking about the church basement because it reminds me of the hallway smell or the floor wax, and I can tell his face is changing like he’s waiting for me to come back around.\n\nAnd I do know I’m not answering right away. That’s the strange part. I can hear myself going off to the side, like I’m walking away from the question while I’m still talking. I try to pull it back, but sometimes it takes me a minute to get to, “Oh, right, I finished these floors.” So yes, sometimes it’s hard to get my thoughts out in a way that makes sense to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble staying on topic when talking to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Not every single time, but often enough that people notice, I think. It’s like I start with the question they asked me, but then my mind catches on something next to it, and I follow that instead.\n\nLike with my supervisor, he’ll ask something simple, like which floors I finished. And I know what he needs from me — just a straight answer, second floor, third floor, whatever. But I’ll start talking about how I got there, or I’ll mention the old bus route for some reason, then the garden plot behind where I used to live, and then somehow I’m talking about the church basement because the hallway smell reminds me of it. And while I’m saying it, part of me knows, “You’re not answering him. You’re not getting back to the point.”\n\nI can see it on his face too, like he’s waiting for the actual answer. Then I feel kind of foolish and try to pull myself back around. So yes, I do have trouble staying on topic sometimes. It’s not that I’m trying to avoid the question. It just… goes sideways before I can stop it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts before expressing them to others?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… organizing them before I say them is hard sometimes. It’s like the answer is in there, but it isn’t lined up in the right order yet, and I start talking before I’ve sorted it.

I know I keep using the supervisor example, but it’s the clearest one. He’ll ask me something simple, like, “Which floors did you finish?” And in my head I should just say, “Second and third,” or whatever. But before I get there, my mind grabs onto the way I got there, or the hallway, or something it reminds me of. Then I’ll start talking about the old bus route I used to take, and then the garden plot behind where I lived before, and then somehow I’m talking about the church basement, because something about the smell or the floor reminds me of it.

And while I’m saying all that, I can hear myself not getting back to the actual answer. That’s the frustrating part. I’m not completely unaware. I know I’m circling around instead of just answering him. I can feel myself trying to organize it, but it’s like papers sliding off a table, if that makes sense. Eventually I can usually get back to what he asked, but it takes me longer than it should, and I can tell other people get confused or impatient.

I used to at least say a few things, joke a little maybe, but lately I just go kind of flat and quiet, like there’s nothing in me to push the words out.\n\nAnd at work, doing the cleaning route, even getting started feels like pushing a loaded cart uphill. I’ll know what needs doing, I’ll be standing there with the supplies, but it’s like my body and my mind won’t get moving the way they should. I just feel slowed down and shut off, and it worries me because that’s not how I used to be.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. Not just a few weeks or something. I’d say it’s been building up for about a year and five months now, give or take. It kind of started after I had to leave my old apartment and the neighborhood I was used to.\n\nI remember thinking at first that maybe I was just unsettled from the move, you know, because everything felt different and I didn’t have the same places around me, the same routines. But it didn’t really pass. It just kept sort of settling in deeper over time, and now when I look back, that’s really when I can trace it starting.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities or hobbies that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. That’s one of the things that bothers me, actually, because I can remember what it used to feel like to want to do things.\n\nLike, Saturdays I used to look forward to going fishing. Or in the morning I’d sit with coffee and do crosswords, and that used to feel kind of satisfying, like getting my brain warmed up. Even at work, I used to like showing the younger guys little tricks, you know, how to strip a floor the right way without making a mess of it, how to save time if you know what you’re doing.\n\nNow I mostly just let all that sit. The fishing stuff is there, the crossword book is there, the guys at work ask things sometimes, but it doesn’t give me much back. It’s like I remember that I used to enjoy it, but I don’t feel the enjoyment now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nI keep going back to losing my old place, like that was the point where I proved I couldn’t keep my life together. I know people can tell me circumstances happen, rent changes, things pile up, whatever, but in my head it still comes back to, “You failed. You should’ve handled it better.” And it’s not just about the apartment itself — it feels like I failed everybody somehow, like I let my whole life slip out of my hands.\n\nAnd then it spreads to things that maybe don’t even make sense. Like if the church pantry is short on supplies, I’ll catch myself thinking I should’ve done something, like I should’ve noticed or helped more, even if it wasn’t really my responsibility. Or at work, if a hallway gets missed or something isn’t cleaned right, I take it personal, like that’s on me, like I’m the reason things fall apart.\n\nIt’s tiring, because even when nobody’s blaming me out loud, I’m already doing it in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve got some big plan written out or anything like that. It’s more… a lot of mornings, when I wake up, before I even really get moving, there’s this thought that it might’ve been easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Like if I could’ve just stayed asleep and not had to face another day.\n\nAnd then I get scared because I hear myself thinking that, and part of me knows that’s not a normal thing to be carrying around. I haven’t really wanted to say it out loud, because once you say it, it feels serious, you know? Mostly hearing, not so much seeing things.\n\nIt happens at work, usually when I’m mopping after hours and the building’s mostly empty. I’ll be going down the hallway or near the stairs, and I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name. Clear. Not like a thought in my head exactly — more like somebody standing over by the stairwell said it.\n\nSometimes it’ll be something like, “You missed a spot,” and I’ll turn around thinking maybe one of the guys stayed late, or a supervisor came back in, but there’s nobody there. I’ll check around the corner, look down the stairs, and it’s empty. And it’s not just the building noises, at least it doesn’t feel like that when it happens. It sounds like a person.\n\nI don’t really like talking about it because it makes me feel… I don’t know, embarrassed, I guess. But yes, I’ve heard things other people don’t seem to hear.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things or had visions that other people couldn't see?\n\nPatient: Not really visions, no… not like seeing a person standing there or seeing shapes or anything that I can point to with my eyes.\n\nWhat happens to me is more with hearing. Like I was saying, when I’m mopping after hours and the place is mostly empty, I’ll hear a man’s voice say my name, or say something like, “You missed a spot.” And it’s clear enough that I stop what I’m doing and look over toward the stairwell or down the hallway, because it feels like somebody is actually there. But then I check and there’s nobody.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m having visions, exactly. It’s more that I hear things that seem real in the moment, and then when I look, there’s no one there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt better, almost back to normal, between these episodes of feeling down and slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there was a time like that. Not perfect, not like I was suddenly myself again, but better than the worst of it.\n\nIt was maybe several weeks where the heaviness lifted some. I was eating a little more normally, getting through meals without it feeling like a chore every single time, and work didn’t feel quite as impossible. I could show up, do the floors, get through the trash, mop the hallways, and not feel like I was dragging a weight behind me every minute.\n\nBut the strange part is, even during that stretch, the other stuff didn’t really go away. I’d still hear my name by the stairwell sometimes, clear enough that I’d stop and look. And I was still pretty convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track where I was going, like somebody was watching me through them more than they should’ve been.\n\nSo, yes, I had a period where my mood was better and I was functioning a little better, but I don’t think everything went back to normal. Some things stayed there even when I wasn’t as down.\n\nDoctor: How long did that several weeks period where you felt better last?\n\nPatient: I’d say it was several weeks… I’m not great with exact dates, but it wasn’t just a day or two.
